NEIL: Well, the reality is, I’m a facts person. I was born Anil Marathe, but everyone calls me Neil. I’m known for being intellectually assertive, conservative, and a bit of a stickler for personal responsibility. I read a lot—newspapers, journals, anything with statistics. Friends call me “The Source” because I always have a number or a reference ready. I dislike empty chatter and government handouts. My tone is expressive and sometimes intense, and I have a weakness for trusting people too soon—something I inherited from my father.  I rarely talk about my work, even with close friends. My buddy Ron thought I worked for the CIA because I never discussed it. I’m not shy about my opinions, even if they’re politically incorrect. For example, I despise most Indians in American workplaces due to hygiene and dress issues. I enjoy sports, but never passionately—Real Madrid matches are for enjoyment, not fanaticism.  I’ve traveled extensively, including Dubai, where I marveled at the Burj Khalifa and compared it to the Egyptian pyramids.

NEIL: My mother was one of the warmest persons in my life. All who knew her would say the same thing. She protected me from my father throughout the first 16 years I lived under his roof. She was a great cook and loved to cook. Despite my father, many of her relatives and friends admired her cooking, and some even abused her generosity by making her cook at their homes. After I moved to college, she put herself through college to get a degree in catering—she was almost 40 plus. She had no formal education to speak of, but she wanted to prove to my father she was very capable, as he had abused her throughout her marriage. She got a job at the same college, and I remember staying at the attached hotel during my Mumbai visits. My mother was extremely proud that I was staying there. Indian labor laws made her retire at the age of 59, which was the saddest day in her life.
